# Pay-source research prompt pack — every unpriced position

Generated by `python scripts/report_unpriced_positions.py` from
`output/graph.json`. Do not edit by hand; regenerate it.

## What this covers

The published graph carries **4,591 position nodes**. **461** carry a pay claim
an official document supports. **4,130** do not, and every one of them is listed
in this pack across **39 shards** — the complete inventory, with each
position's id and the reason nothing reached it, is in `docs/UNPRICED_POSITIONS.md`.

Why each is unpriced today:

- **3,316** — no source this project has read names the title at all. Not a coverage gap somebody has not got to: no pay document in the repository names this title under this organisation, so nothing could have reached it.
- **793** — the node states a multiplicity (×N) and this project refuses to put one rate on it. A rate beside a panel describing a whole group reads as what one holder earns. Knowing which pay system governs the title is still useful -- it is what would let the graph carry the schedule rather than a rate -- so these are listed and asked about.
- **21** — OPM lists the position and the row prints no rate. The PLUM archive or the current export names the title under this organisation but the Level/Grade/Pay cell carries a rank or is empty, and no salary table this project has read prices that pay plan.

## How to run this pack

Run **Prompt 0** first and read the answer before running any shard: it decides
which pay systems exist and where each is published, and a shard answer that
contradicts it is a finding rather than an update. Then run the shards in any
order; they are independent.

Prompt 0 carries the follow-up-chain directive because it is exploratory. The
shards do not, deliberately: they ask for one line per title across hundreds of
titles, and an expansion pass there buries the per-title verdicts the shard
exists to produce. Each shard ends in its own LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS list.

**Nothing an answer returns is published by this project until a matcher reads
it out of a committed document.** A research answer names a document to fetch;
it is never itself the citation.

---

## Prompt 0 — the structural question

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government in which
every published figure must be traceable to a specific published government
document that prints it. I need to price federal POSITIONS (job titles), not
agencies.

Question: what is the complete set of pay systems under which U.S. federal
civilian and uniformed positions are compensated, and for EACH system:

1. Which statute or authority establishes it?
2. Which agency publishes the current pay schedule, and at what exact URL?
3. Does that published schedule state a single annual RATE per position, a
   RANGE (minimum/maximum or steps), or no figure at all?
4. What is the join key a matcher would use to find one position's row in it
   (grade, level, tier, series, statutory citation, printed title)?
5. Which categories of federal position are NOT covered by any published
   schedule, so that no official document states what they are paid?

Be exhaustive about (5) in particular. I need to know where the honest answer
is “no document states this”, because publishing an estimate there would be a
false claim, and I would rather publish nothing.

Prefer .gov primary sources. Flag every number as official-published vs
third-party-estimated. Do not cite salary-aggregator sites (FederalPay.org,
GovSalaries, Glassdoor and similar) as authorities — name them only to say a
figure is a third-party republication.

FOLLOW-UP CHAIN DIRECTIVE:
After answering the question above in full, continue as follows:

LEVEL 1 — State the 3 most decision-relevant follow-up questions your answer
raises for this tool, and answer each with sources.

LEVEL 2 — For each Level-1 answer that materially affects a design decision,
pose and answer the single most important follow-up it raises, with sources.

LEVEL 3 — Repeat once more for any Level-2 answer that still carries open
decision weight.

BUDGET: no more than 10 follow-up answers total across all levels. Prune by
decision-relevance, not curiosity — drop branches that only add color.

For EVERY follow-up answer:
(a) open with one line stating why this follow-up matters for the tool,
(b) cite primary sources,
(c) flag each number as official-published vs third-party-estimated.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: a flat list of every number
in this entire response that a design decision might rest on — one line per
number, with its source. This list feeds an independent verification pass.
```

---

## Prompt 1 — 2 organisation(s), 109 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 2 — 4 organisation(s), 96 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.
```

## Prompt 3 — 4 organisation(s), 96 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.
```

## Prompt 5 — 4 organisation(s), 96 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 8 — 6 organisation(s), 96 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 10 — 9 organisation(s), 109 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 12 — 10 organisation(s), 110 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 16 — 12 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 18 — 13 organisation(s), 104 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each

DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
```

## Prompt 20 — 15 organisation(s), 107 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 23 — 15 organisation(s), 105 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 25 — 18 organisation(s), 106 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```

```
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
```

```
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency

4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
```

## Prompt 28 — 27 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```

## Prompt 29 — 27 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 31 — 27 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```

## Prompt 32 — 27 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 34 — 36 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```

## Prompt 35 — 36 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```

## Prompt 36 — 36 organisation(s), 108 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```

## Prompt 37 — 46 organisation(s), 110 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 38 — 55 organisation(s), 110 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.
```

## Prompt 39 — 66 organisation(s), 106 title(s)

```
I am building a data-backed public graph of the U.S. federal government. Every
published figure must be traceable to a specific published government document
that prints it; I cannot publish a reported or estimated salary.

Below are federal POSITIONS, grouped by the organisation they sit in. For each
one I need to know what it is paid and, more importantly, WHICH PUBLISHED
DOCUMENT states that.

For EVERY title listed below, return exactly one line in this pipe-delimited
format and nothing else per title:

    <node id> | <pay system> | <document URL> | <rate|range|none> | <join key> | <figure or —> | <confidence>

- **pay system** — one of: `general_schedule`, `senior_executive_service`,
  `senior_level`, `executive_schedule`, `title_38_va`, `title_5_excepted`,
  `administratively_determined`, `foreign_service`, `military_title_37`,
  `federal_wage_system`, `judicial_statutory`, `legislative_chamber`,
  `board_or_commission_statutory`, `not_federally_paid`, `unknown`.
- **document URL** — the URL of the *published document that states the pay*,
  on the publisher's own site. A `.gov` host wherever one exists. Not a news
  article, not a salary-aggregator site, not Wikipedia, not FederalPay.org or
  GovSalaries — those are third-party republications and this tool cannot cite
  them. If no official document states it, write `—` and say so.
- **rate|range|none** — whether that document prints a single annual rate,
  a minimum-and-maximum band, or no figure at all for this title.
- **join key** — the exact string the document uses for this title, so a
  matcher can find the row: a grade (`GS-15`), a level (`EX-IV`), a printed
  title, a tier name, a statutory citation.
- **figure** — only where the document prints one, as printed. `—` otherwise.
  Never estimate, never average, never interpolate between grades.
- **confidence** — `certain` only if you opened the document and read the row;
  `likely` if the pay system is documented but the specific row is inferred;
  `speculative` otherwise.

Rules that matter more than coverage:
1. **A missing answer is a result.** A line reading
   `<node id> | unknown | — | none | — | — | certain` is a correct answer and
   is more useful than a guess. Do not fill gaps.
2. **Never average or interpolate.** If a title spans GS-13 to GS-15, say
   `range` and give the grade span as the join key — do not produce a midpoint.
3. **Do not read across agencies.** An Inspector General's pay at one agency
   is not evidence about another's; answer per organisation as listed.
4. A title marked `[×N]` stands for several holders in this graph. Answer the
   pay system and document for it anyway; do not produce a single figure.

END with a section titled LOAD-BEARING NUMBERS: every figure you returned
above, one line each, with the document URL it came from and whether it is
official-published or third-party-estimated.

THE TITLES:
```
